=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums of length n and a 2D array queries where queries[i] = [l_i, r_i].
Each queries[i] represents the following action on nums:

Decrement the value at each index in the range [l_i, r_i] in nums by at most 1.
The amount by which the value is decremented can be chosen independently for each index.

A Zero Array is an array with all its elements equal to 0.
Return the maximum number of elements that can be removed from queries, such that nums can still be converted to a zero array using the remaining queries. If it is not possible to convert nums to a zero array, return -1.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [2,0,2], queries = [[0,2],[0,2],[1,1]]
Output: 1
Explanation:
After removing queries[2], nums can still be converted to a zero array.

Using queries[0], decrement nums[0] and nums[2] by 1 and nums[1] by 0.
Using queries[1], decrement nums[0] and nums[2] by 1 and nums[1] by 0.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [1,1,1,1], queries = [[1,3],[0,2],[1,3],[1,2]]
Output: 2
Explanation:
We can remove queries[2] and queries[3].

Example 3:

Input: nums = [1,2,3,4], queries = [[0,3]]
Output: -1
Explanation:
nums cannot be converted to a zero array even after using all the queries.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
0 <= nums[i] <= 10^5
1 <= queries.length <= 10^5
queries[i].length == 2
0 <= l_i <= r_i < nums.length

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxRemoval(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Implementation details:
```
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def maxRemoval(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        by_l = [[] for _ in range(n)]
        for l,r in queries:
            by_l[l].append(r)
        available = []  # max-heap of r, store -r
        active = []     # min-heap of r selected
        cnt = 0
        selected = 0
        for i in range(n):
            for r in by_l[i]:
                heapq.heappush(available, -r)
            # expire selected intervals
            while active and active[0] < i:
                heapq.heappop(active)
                cnt -= 1
            # discard expired available intervals
            while available and -available[0] < i:
                heapq.heappop(available)
            while cnt < nums[i]:
                # remove expired available (already done, but after selecting? no new expired)
                while available and -available[0] < i:
                    heapq.heappop(available)
                if not available:
                    return -1
                r = -heapq.heappop(available)
                # r should >= i
                cnt += 1
                selected += 1
                heapq.heappush(active, r)
        return len(queries) - selected
```

Classic problem: Given intervals, choose min to cover each point at least demand. Greedy at each point if current coverage < demand, choose interval with farthest right endpoint among intervals covering current point. This is optimal. However, we are in an inductive greedy proof where previous points are already satisfied by greedy selections; we need maintain feasibility for future and current, not necessarily for past because past already processed. But if replacing in optimal solution for remaining problem, we can restrict to suffix from i onward. For points < i, greedy already selected intervals; optimal for remaining can be combined? Need a standard proof.

Let's formalize: At step i, before ensuring demand at i, greedy has selected some intervals G_prev. It maintains that there exists an optimal solution (for whole problem) that includes G_prev? We can prove by induction. Suppose there is an optimal solution O containing G_prev. It contains some interval h covering i not in G_prev (maybe more). Let g be greedy's chosen farthest r among available intervals not selected? Therefore replacing h with g preserves feasibility for all points >= i and previous points due to G_prev. So there is optimal containing g. This proves greedy.

Now, let's examine the code style. We need define class Solution with method maxRemoval. Use `from typing import List` and `import heapq`. The final answer code block should include these imports. Let's write final code:

```python
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def maxRemoval(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        by_left = [[] for _ in range(n)]
        for l, r in queries:
            by_left[l].append(r)
        
        available = []  # max-heap by r, store -r
        active = []     # min-heap of r for selected queries
        active_count = 0
        kept = 0
        
        for i in range(n):
            for r in by_left[i]:
                heapq.heappush(available, -r)
            
            while active and active[0] < i:
                heapq.heappop(active)
                active_count -= 1
            
            while available and -available[0] < i:
                heapq.heappop(available)
            
            while active_count < nums[i]:
                while available and -available[0] < i:
                    heapq.heappop(available)
                if not available:
                    return -1
                r = -heapq.heappop(available)
                active_count += 1
                kept += 1
                heapq.heappush(active, r)
        
        return len(queries) - kept
```

So expired intervals are popped in decreasing r order over time, not necessarily at exact expiration. But they are popped before they can be selected because selection checks top. Good. Memory O(m). Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "nums" values can be 0 and active_count >0; if active_count > nums[i], we don't select, but we also don't "unselect" intervals. Could this lead to selecting more intervals than necessary? Greedy only selects when active_count < nums[i]. If active_count > nums[i], no selection. If active_count was high due to previous selections, those previous selections were necessary at their time. Could an optimal solution have selected fewer previous intervals but still satisfy previous demands by selecting intervals that expire before i, resulting in lower active_count now and maybe need more later? But total count? Let's examine more deeply with a potential counterexample to greedy optimality.

We need be absolutely sure. Let's attempt to find a counterexample via reasoning.

Problem: min cardinality subset of intervals with coverage >= demand. Greedy: sweep left to right, maintain selected active intervals. At each position i, if active_count < demand[i], select intervals with largest r among intervals with l <= i and r >= i until active_count = demand[i]. This is a known algorithm for "minimum number of intervals to cover points with multiplicity" (also called "minimum intervals to satisfy coverage requirements"). It is optimal. Let's prove by induction more rigorously.

Let G be greedy selected set after processing up to i-1. Inductive hypothesis: There exists an optimal solution O that contains G. At position i, let c = coverage of G at i. If c >= nums[i], then O containing G works, continue. If c < nums[i], then O must contain at least nums[i] - c intervals covering i that are not in G. Let h be one of them. Let g be the interval greedy selects (with maximum r among all available unselected intervals covering i). Since h is available and covers i, r_g >= r_h. Also l_g <= i (available). Replace h by g in O. Need show O' is feasible and contains G ∪ {g}. For positions < i: G already covers demands (by greedy construction). h may have covered some positions < i, but removing h could reduce coverage. However, G alone covers demands for positions < i, so O' still has G plus other intervals; coverage at positions < i is at least coverage of G, which is >= demand. Wait is G alone covering demands for all positions < i? Greedy selected intervals to ensure at each previous position p, after processing p, active_count >= nums[p]. But active_count at p included intervals selected up to p. Later selected intervals (after p) have l > p, so they don't cover p. Intervals selected before p that expire before p? They wouldn't have been active at p. Thus the set of intervals selected up to p covers p. Since G includes all selected up to i-1, it includes those. So G alone covers all positions < i. Good.
For position i: O' has g instead of h. Both cover i, so coverage at i unchanged (or plus if h=g? if h not g). For positions > i: If h covers t, then h.l <= i <= t and h.r >= t. g.l <= i <= t and g.r >= h.r >= t, so g covers t. Thus coverage at t is at least as high as O (actually replacing h with g doesn't decrease coverage at any t >= i). Therefore O' feasible. It contains G ∪ {g}. Thus induction. This proves greedy optimal.

Need note: What if O contains multiple intervals covering i and replacing one h with g could cause O' to have duplicate g? If O already contains g, then O contains G∪{g} and no replacement needed. If O doesn't contain g, h exists and h != g. Good.
What if g is already in G? Then O contains G, so contains g; no need to select? But greedy selects g only if not selected. It pops from available, so not in G. Good.

Thus algorithm correct.

=== ANSWER ===
must be final code. Need output final Python code only. I've already reasoned enough. Need ensure code is correct.

Let's produce final code.</think>```python
from typing import List
import heapq

class Solution:
    def maxRemoval(self, nums: List[int], queries: List[List[int]]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        by_left = [[] for _ in range(n)]
        for l, r in queries:
            by_left[l].append(r)

        available = []  # max-heap by r, stored as -r
        active = []     # min-heap of r for selected queries
        active_count = 0
        kept = 0

        for i in range(n):
            for r in by_left[i]:
                heapq.heappush(available, -r)

            # Remove selected intervals that no longer cover i.
            while active and active[0] < i:
                heapq.heappop(active)
                active_count -= 1

            # Discard unused intervals that already ended.
            while available and -available[0] < i:
                heapq.heappop(available)

            # Add queries until the current position is covered enough.
            while active_count < nums[i]:
                while available and -available[0] < i:
                    heapq.heappop(available)

                if not available:
                    return -1

                r = -heapq.heappop(available)
                active_count += 1
                kept += 1
                heapq.heappush(active, r)

        return len(queries) - kept
```
